{
  "file_path": "/Users/anton/src/drain-cycle/docs/designs/end-to-end-skill-drain.md",
  "content": "# Drain a project end-to-end on composable skills — supervised, not babysat\n\n**Status:** north-star design doc · **Audience:** Anton (operator/developer) · **Repos:** `drain-cycle` (Python supervisor) + `agent-skills-shaper` (the Shaper skill pack).\n\nThis is the umbrella the project-level work serves. It supersedes the architecture content scattered across `docs/initiative-multi-agent-collaboration.md` (the dispatcher framing) and `agent-skills-shaper/docs/ideas/lifecycle-expansion.md` (the process/workflow split) — both are now facets of the single picture below. When a project and this doc disagree, this doc is the one to change first, deliberately; the projects follow it.\n\n---\n\n## 1. Problem\n\nI want one toolbox of capabilities that I can use two ways without rebuilding anything:\n\n- **By hand** — invoke a single discrete skill at the keyboard when I want exactly that one step (`/shape:design`, `/exec:review`, `/shape:pr-finishing`).\n- **Unattended** — point an engine at a Linear project and have it run those same skills in the right sequence, ticket by ticket, to a merged reviewable PR — without me watching every step.\n\nToday neither half is clean. The skills exist but assume a human is driving; the automation (`drain-cycle`) re-implements the workflow as prompt prose instead of pointing at the skills; and the two have started to diverge. The failure that makes this concrete: a worker can finish the *work* and still not finish the *ticket*, and nothing catches it (§7).\n\nThe desired end-state: the **same skills** are the unit of work in both modes, and the automation is a thin layer that does only what a watching human would otherwise do — start the work, hold the guardrails, and check the result.\n\n## 2. Context and constraints\n\n**Where this came from.** `drain-cycle` began as \"drain a Linear cycle unattended — one ticket, one worker, one diff.\" Three threads then pulled it forward: the Shaper pack grew an execution half (the `exec:*` skills); a re-scope decision said the pack should *own* the workflow and drain-cycle should shrink to a supervisor (`lifecycle-expansion.md` §2); and a real incident (§7) showed the supervisor trusting the worker too much.\n\n**Prior decisions this inherits** (`docs/design-decisions.md`): §1 the spawned agent updates Linear itself; §10 headless workers inherit project config by symlink — *this is what makes a skill behave identically by hand and inside a worker*; §14 halted issues resume by reusing the worktree; §16–18 the Graphite stack-mode + handoff contract. Decision §17 (\"`/shape:task` runs inside the worker session\") is the high-water mark of the *old* inline-workflow approach and is superseded by the boundary below.\n\n**Non-functional requirements** (each with a fitness function — the automated check that keeps it true):\n\n| NFR | Target | Fitness function |\n|-----|--------|------------------|\n| Boundary purity (supervisor carries no workflow) | 0 workflow-procedure verbs (review/fix/commit/finish sequences) in `drain_cycle/prompt.py`; 0 `gh`/`gt` calls in `orchestrator.py` | `grep` check in the test suite |\n| Pointer-only prompt | worker prompt template ≤ 15 lines | `wc -l` on the template + procedure-verb grep (supervisor project KR3) |\n| Recording completeness | 100% of verify-flow `Done` run-log entries carry an `outcome_verdict` | `drain-cycle grade` exits non-zero on any violation (correctness project KR2) |\n| Dual-mode parity | every execution skill runs to completion both invoked by hand and inside a worker, with no orchestrator-only assumption | per-skill self-trial on both surfaces (execution project KR1) |\n| End-to-end autonomy | ≥ 4 of 5 hard tickets reach a merged PR with zero operator fix commits | `drain-cycle grade` + `git log` post-push-fix scan (correctness project KR1) |\n\n## 3. The architecture: two layers, one artifact boundary\n\n\"Orchestration\" was doing two jobs under one word. Split them and the inconsistency dissolves.\n\n**Layer 2 — Workflow (the intra-issue layer). Owned by the Shaper pack.**\nThe content-aware sequence of roles inside one ticket: pickup → breakdown → build → review → verify → prepare PR → finish/submit → respond to feedback. It knows the order and the gates. It is already built: `exec:pickup` is the entry skill that delegates each step to its owning skill (`exec:breakdown` / `exec:build` / `exec:review` / `exec:verify` / `exec:finish`). Crucially, every step is *also* a skill you can invoke on its own — the sequence is a skill that calls skills, not a monolith.\n\n**Layer 1 — Supervision (the session layer). Owned by drain-cycle.**\nThe content-blind, vendor-agnostic process concerns: spawn a worker session, enforce guardrails (token/turn caps, timeouts), halt on breach, revert Linear state, resume across runs, recover stranded committed work, record the run-log, grade. It never knows what \"review\" or \"verify\" *means*. It only asks: *Is the issue Done? Is there an `outcome_verdict`? Does `.drain-handoff.json` carry `pr_urls`?*\n\n**The boundary is the artifact.** The two layers never call into each other — they communicate only through durable signals: **Linear state, run-log fields, and the `.drain-handoff.json` handoff file.** The workflow *writes* them; the supervisor *reads* them. That is the entire contract.\n\n```\n                         ┌──────────────── Layer 1: drain-cycle (supervision) ─────────────────┐\n   manual mode           │  spawn · guardrails · halt · revert · resume · recover · record ·   │\n   (you, by hand) ─┐     │  grade        — reads artifacts only, never the workflow's steps    │\n                   │     └───────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────┘\n                   │            reads ▲   writes      │ spawns a worker that runs ▼\n                   ▼                  │   (run-log,   │\n        ┌─────────────────────────────│───Linear,────│────────────────────────────────────────┐\n        │  Layer 2: Shaper pack (workflow)  handoff)  ▼                                         │\n        │  exec:pickup → breakdown → build → review → verify → prepare → finish → respond       │\n        │  every box is also a standalone skill you can invoke alone                            │\n        └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘\n```\n\n**The placement test** — for any piece of work, ask: *does this require drain-cycle to know what a role does (Layer 2 → pack), or only whether an artifact exists (Layer 1 → supervisor)?* That single question routes every ticket and prevents the layers from leaking into each other again.\n\n**Why dual-mode falls out for free.** Because Layer 2 is just skills and Layer 1 only reads artifacts, the skills carry no dependency on the supervisor. Invoked by hand they do the same thing; driven by a worker they do the same thing. drain-cycle is automation *on top of* the toolbox, never a precondition for it.\n\n## 4. Alternatives considered\n\n- **Do nothing** — keep the workflow inlined in `prompt.py` and duplicated in the pack. *Blast radius:* the two definitions drift (already happening — the prompt's `/shape:task` directive points at a superseded skill); the §7 failure class persists; no manual reuse of the workflow. *Reversal cost:* cheap today, rising with every divergence.\n- **Fat supervisor** — drain-cycle inlines all role logic in Python. *Blast radius:* vendor lock-in (only a Claude worker can follow it), no by-hand reuse, every skill change becomes a Python change, prompt bloat. *Reversal cost:* high — unwinding role logic out of the orchestrator.\n- **No supervisor** — skills self-supervise (each enforces its own caps/halt/resume). *Blast radius:* content-aware code doing process control; can't enforce cross-session token caps or resume after a crash, because a single skill can't see outside its own session. *Reversal cost:* high.\n- **Recommended: two layers split on the artifact boundary** (§3), skills dual-mode, drain-cycle an optional supervisor. Beats the alternatives on every NFR in §2 and is the only one that delivers both use-modes from one definition.\n\n## 5. Recommended approach\n\nAdopt the §3 split as the invariant. Concretely:\n\n1. **Strip `prompt.py` to a pointer.** The worker prompt becomes \"work issue X in worktree Y; follow `exec:pickup`\" (≤ 15 lines, no procedure). The workflow lives once, in the pack.\n2. **Keep the supervisor content-blind.** Its only decisions read artifacts: Done-state, `outcome_verdict`, `pr_urls`. Halting, reverting, resuming, recovering, grading — all keyed off those, never off the workflow's internal steps.\n3. **Make every execution skill dual-mode by construction.** No skill assumes an orchestrator is present; the supervisor supplies only context (issue, worktree, base) and reads back artifacts.\n4. **Treat the artifact contract as the API between repos.** Changes to run-log fields / handoff schema are versioned interface changes, reviewed as such.\n\n## 6. The verdict-crossing seam (the load-bearing unbuilt piece)\n\nThe one genuinely new integration surface — and a likely one-way door, because it sets the handoff/run-log schema everything downstream reads.\n\nLayer 2 produces verdicts: `outcome_verdict` (from `exec:verify`), `prep_verdict` (from `pr-prepare`/`exec:finish`). Layer 1's enforcement and grading need them. But **today `handoff.py` carries only `pr_urls`** — there is no channel for a verdict to cross the boundary back into the run-log. Until there is, the supervisor can recover *unfinished* work (it can see \"no `pr_urls`\") but cannot act on *rejected* work (it can't see a FAIL verdict), and the grader can't enforce \"every Done has a recorded verdict.\"\n\nThis seam must be designed before the correctness work proceeds. The decision: **extend the handoff file as the single verdict-carrying artifact** the workflow writes and the supervisor reads — `{pr_urls, outcome_verdict, prep_verdict, ...}` — versus separate channels per verdict. Either way the schema is the contract; pick it deliberately (Open Question 1).\n\n## 7. Grounding incident\n\nCycle `793fc9ea`, issue **ABA-383**: a worker pinned to `model:haiku` committed 4 reviewable slices, then exited *without* running review → `/shape:pr-finishing` → handoff → Done. The supervisor's not-Done halt reverted the issue and **stranded the committed work**. This is the architecture's motivating example: the worker did the Layer-2 work but skipped the finishing steps, and the Layer-1 supervisor — which should enforce completion from the outside — only knew how to halt, not recover. **ABA-393** (orchestrator-enforced completion: a sonnet finishing sub-agent recovers committed-but-unfinished work, opus for fixes) is the Layer-1 answer, and it is pure supervision: it reads \"commits exist, but not Done / no `pr_urls`\" and acts, never touching what the workflow means.\n\n## 8. How the projects map onto this design\n\n| Linear project | Layer | Status | What it is under this design |\n|----------------|-------|--------|------------------------------|\n| Issues drain end-to-end on first-party skills | L2 | Completed | Built the `exec:*` workflow skills — the reason L2 already exists |\n| Consolidate Shaper's Lifecycle into Four Phase Skills | L2 | In progress | Consolidates the shaping-side skills (idea/project/design/delivery) |\n| **drain-cycle supervises; the pack owns the workflow** | boundary | Planned | **Keystone — run first.** Strips `prompt.py` to a pointer, establishes the L1/L2 boundary |\n| Multi-agent collaboration for correctness | L1 | Backlog | **Reframe** as the L1 enforcement+recording+grading project (see below) |\n| Drain past the merge gate | L1 | idea | The supervisor's autonomy horizon: optimistic stacking → trust-tiered auto-merge → resident daemon. All Layer 1 (process), gated behind the boundary landing first |\n\n**Reframing \"Multi-agent collaboration for correctness.\"** It predates the boundary, so its six-role *skill-building* is overtaken — those skills now exist in the pack. Split its tickets by the §3 placement test:\n\n- **Keep (Layer 1 — genuinely unbuilt supervision):** recording fields ABA-320/321/322; verifier-gated-Done enforcement ABA-328 + orchestrator recovery ABA-393 (two branches of one post-check — see §6/§7); grading ABA-333/334/336; the one missing Layer-2 skill, PR Responder + the `pr-feedback` subcommand ABA-332 (build the skill into the pack, the subcommand into drain-cycle).\n- **Close or fold (overtaken — Layer-2 skill-build / worker-wiring that the boundary replaces):** ABA-324/326/327/330.\n\nIts KRs survive almost unchanged because they were already phrased as artifact checks (\"every Done has a recorded `outcome_verdict`\") — Layer-1 by construction. Only the implementation had drifted into the worker.\n\n## 9. Consequences\n\n- **Walking skeleton = the keystone.** The boundary re-scope (strip `prompt.py`, point at `exec:pickup`) must land before anything else; it is the seam the whole design rests on, and it carries the real integration risk (a pointer-only prompt must drain a real ticket as well as the inlined one did). Everything else is additive once it holds.\n- **Positive:** one definition serves both use-modes; a non-Claude worker can follow the same prose (vendor-agnostic); ABA-383-class recovery becomes natural Layer-1 behaviour; each layer is independently testable.\n- **Negative:** the verdict-crossing seam is new surface and a schema one-way door (§6); two repos must stay in sync through the artifact contract, which is now a real interface to version.\n\n## 10. Operability\n\nThe design is observable through the same artifacts that *are* the boundary, so enforcement is mechanical, not manual:\n\n- **Metrics / logs:** the run-log entry per worker exit (exit state, `final_linear_state`, `halt_reason`, the verdict fields once §6 lands) and the OTel `drain.issue` span attributes.\n- **Fitness functions:** the five NFR checks in §2 — boundary-purity grep, prompt-line cap, `drain-cycle grade` exit code, dual-mode self-trial, end-to-end autonomy scan. These are the alerts; a red fitness function is the signal the design has drifted.\n- **Rollback:** the keystone re-scope has a defined fallback — if a pointer-only prompt causes 3 consecutive halted drains the inlined prompt would have completed, restore the inline tail and keep the re-scope to guardrails+logging only (the supervisor project's kill condition). ABA-393 recovery is bounded to one attempt per issue per run.\n- **Failure modes:** worker skips finishing (→ L1 recovery, §7); verdict can't cross (→ §6 blocks correctness work, not the drain itself); restack conflict on merged upstream (→ halt-and-park, per *Drain past the merge gate*).\n\n## 11. Open questions\n\n1. **Verdict-crossing schema (one-way door).** Does the handoff file become the single verdict-carrying artifact (`{pr_urls, outcome_verdict, prep_verdict, …}`), or do verdicts cross by separate channels? *Owner:* Anton. *Resolution gate:* the keystone re-scope design / the reframed recording milestone — blocks all Layer-1 correctness enforcement.\n2. **Execution-skill verb namespace.** `exec:*` (current) vs `ship:*` (proposed in `lifecycle-expansion.md`). *Owner:* Anton. *Gate:* before the PR Responder skill is authored.\n3. **Autonomy host.** Does end-to-end autonomy (Drain past the merge gate) run under a `/loop`/cron relaunch interim, or wait for the resident daemon? *Owner:* Anton. *Gate:* Gear-3 decision, after trust-tiered auto-merge ships.\n4. **Where dual-mode parity is tested.** What fixture proves a skill behaves identically by hand and inside a worker? *Owner:* Anton. *Gate:* the execution project's fixture harness.\n"
}
```

Now I've read both the persona and the doc. Let me apply the writing-editor rubric. This is a design doc, audience is the author himself.

The persona judges prose only: clarity, active voice, no AI filler, nominalizations, labels-vs-achievements, name-the-actor-with-a-verb. Not plan soundness.

---

## Verdict: **accept with notes**

The doc is genuinely strong prose — active voice throughout, headings already carry achievements ("Drain a project end-to-end…", "The verdict-crossing seam (the load-bearing unbuilt piece)"), actors are named with verbs, and the placement test is crisp. No Phase-1–3 load-bearing span fails. What remains is Phase-4 polish: a few nominalizations, two padded openers, and one heading that's a bare label. None block; all are quick verbatim swaps.

A clean re-scan confirms the findings below are the full set worth your time on an already-polished doc.

---

## Findings (prioritized)

**1. §10 heading "Operability" is a label, not an achievement.** (highest value — it's the one heading that breaks the doc's own pattern)
- Before: `## 10. Operability`
- After: `## 10. Enforce the boundary mechanically, not by hand` — or `## 10. The artifacts that are the boundary also monitor it`

The body's lead sentence already states the achievement ("enforcement is mechanical, not manual"); promote it.

**2. §2 nominalization "Boundary purity (supervisor carries no workflow)" in the NFR table.**
- Before: `Boundary purity (supervisor carries no workflow)`
- After: `Supervisor carries no workflow` — drop the nominal "purity"; the parenthetical already says it plainly, so lead with it.

**3. §11 Q1 nominalization "Verdict-crossing schema".** Same shape as the §6 heading, which earns its keep by naming the seam; here a verb reads sharper.
- Before: `**Verdict-crossing schema (one-way door).** Does the handoff file become the single verdict-carrying artifact…`
- After: `**How does a verdict cross the boundary? (one-way door)** Does the handoff file become the single verdict-carrying artifact…`

**4. §9 opener "Walking skeleton = the keystone" leans on jargon + an equals sign where a verb is stronger.**
- Before: `**Walking skeleton = the keystone.** The boundary re-scope (strip \`prompt.py\`, point at \`exec:pickup\`) must land before anything else;`
- After: `**The keystone lands first.** The boundary re-scope — strip \`prompt.py\`, point at \`exec:pickup\` — must land before anything else;`

**5. §3 sub-heading "Why dual-mode falls out for free" — mild filler ("for free").**
- Before: `**Why dual-mode falls out for free.**`
- After: `**Dual-mode falls out of the split.**`

Keep it; just trade the throwaway "for free" for the actor (the split) doing the work.

**6. §1 padded qualifier "Today neither half is clean."** Minor — "clean" is vague for a sentence that then lists three concrete failures.
- Before: `Today neither half is clean. The skills exist but assume a human is driving;`
- After: `Today both halves are broken. The skills exist but assume a human is driving;`

Lower priority — "clean" is defensible shorthand and you fix it immediately with specifics.

---

Net: six Phase-4 swaps, no rejections. Finding 1 (the bare "Operability" heading) is the only one I'd treat as near-mandatory, since it's the single place the doc violates its own heading discipline.

Eleven sections, following the design-doc discipline (Problem → Context+NFRs → Architecture → Alternatives → Recommended → the unbuilt seam → incident → project map → Consequences → Operability → Open questions), passed a writing-editor review (accept with notes, all applied).

The spine:

- **Dual-mode falls out of the split** — that's the "discrete skills *or* automated engine" vision made structural.

1	# Drain a project end-to-end on composable skills — supervised, not babysat
2	
3	**Status:** north-star design doc · **Audience:** Anton (operator/developer) · **Repos:** `drain-cycle` (Python supervisor) + `agent-skills-shaper` (the Shaper skill pack).
4	
5	This is the umbrella the project-level work serves. It supersedes the architecture content scattered across `docs/initiative-multi-agent-collaboration.md` (the dispatcher framing) and `agent-skills-shaper/docs/ideas/lifecycle-expansion.md` (the process/workflow split) — both are now facets of the single picture below. When a project and this doc disagree, this doc is the one to change first, deliberately; the projects follow it.
6	
7	---
8	
9	## 1. Problem
10	
11	I want one toolbox of capabilities that I can use two ways without rebuilding anything:
12	
13	- **By hand** — invoke a single discrete skill at the keyboard when I want exactly that one step (`/shape:design`, `/exec:review`, `/shape:pr-finishing`).
14	- **Unattended** — point an engine at a Linear project and have it run those same skills in the right sequence, ticket by ticket, to a merged reviewable PR — without me watching every step.
15	
16	Today both halves are broken. The skills exist but assume a human is driving; the automation (`drain-cycle`) re-implements the workflow as prompt prose instead of pointing at the skills; and the two have started to diverge. The failure that makes this concrete: a worker can finish the *work* and still not finish the *ticket*, and nothing catches it (§7).
17	
18	The desired end-state: the **same skills** are the unit of work in both modes, and the automation is a thin layer that does only what a watching human would otherwise do — start the work, hold the guardrails, and check the result.
19	
20	## 2. Context and constraints
21	
22	**Where this came from.** `drain-cycle` began as "drain a Linear cycle unattended — one ticket, one worker, one diff." Three threads then pulled it forward: the Shaper pack grew an execution half (the `exec:*` skills); a re-scope decision said the pack should *own* the workflow and drain-cycle should shrink to a supervisor (`lifecycle-expansion.md` §2); and a real incident (§7) showed the supervisor trusting the worker too much.
23	
24	**Prior decisions this inherits** (`docs/design-decisions.md`): §1 the spawned agent updates Linear itself; §10 headless workers inherit project config by symlink — *this is what makes a skill behave identically by hand and inside a worker*; §14 halted issues resume by reusing the worktree; §16–18 the Graphite stack-mode + handoff contract. Decision §17 ("`/shape:task` runs inside the worker session") is the high-water mark of the *old* inline-workflow approach and is superseded by the boundary below.
25	
26	**Non-functional requirements** (each with a fitness function — the automated check that keeps it true):
27	
28	| NFR | Target | Fitness function |
29	|-----|--------|------------------|
30	| Supervisor carries no workflow | 0 workflow-procedure verbs (review/fix/commit/finish sequences) in `drain_cycle/prompt.py`; 0 `gh`/`gt` calls in `orchestrator.py` | `grep` check in the test suite |
31	| Pointer-only prompt | worker prompt template ≤ 15 lines | `wc -l` on the template + procedure-verb grep (supervisor project KR3) |
32	| Recording completeness | 100% of verify-flow `Done` run-log entries carry an `outcome_verdict` | `drain-cycle grade` exits non-zero on any violation (correctness project KR2) |
33	| Dual-mode parity | every execution skill runs to completion both invoked by hand and inside a worker, with no orchestrator-only assumption | per-skill self-trial on both surfaces (execution project KR1) |
34	| End-to-end autonomy | ≥ 4 of 5 hard tickets reach a merged PR with zero operator fix commits | `drain-cycle grade` + `git log` post-push-fix scan (correctness project KR1) |
35	
36	## 3. The architecture: two layers, one artifact boundary
37	
38	"Orchestration" was doing two jobs under one word. Split them and the inconsistency dissolves.
39	
40	**Layer 2 — Workflow (the intra-issue layer). Owned by the Shaper pack.**
41	The content-aware sequence of roles inside one ticket: pickup → breakdown → build → review → verify → prepare PR → finish/submit → respond to feedback. It knows the order and the gates. It is already built: `exec:pickup` is the entry skill that delegates each step to its owning skill (`exec:breakdown` / `exec:build` / `exec:review` / `exec:verify` / `exec:finish`). Crucially, every step is *also* a skill you can invoke on its own — the sequence is a skill that calls skills, not a monolith.
42	
43	**Layer 1 — Supervision (the session layer). Owned by drain-cycle.**
44	The content-blind, vendor-agnostic process concerns: spawn a worker session, enforce guardrails (token/turn caps, timeouts), halt on breach, revert Linear state, resume across runs, recover stranded committed work, record the run-log, grade. It never knows what "review" or "verify" *means*. It only asks: *Is the issue Done? Is there an `outcome_verdict`? Does `.drain-handoff.json` carry `pr_urls`?*
45	
46	**The boundary is the artifact.** The two layers never call into each other — they communicate only through durable signals: **Linear state, run-log fields, and the `.drain-handoff.json` handoff file.** The workflow *writes* them; the supervisor *reads* them. That is the entire contract.
47	
48	```
49	                         ┌──────────────── Layer 1: drain-cycle (supervision) ─────────────────┐
50	   manual mode           │  spawn · guardrails · halt · revert · resume · recover · record ·   │
51	   (you, by hand) ─┐     │  grade        — reads artifacts only, never the workflow's steps    │
52	                   │     └───────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────┘
53	                   │            reads ▲   writes      │ spawns a worker that runs ▼
54	                   ▼                  │   (run-log,   │
55	        ┌─────────────────────────────│───Linear,────│────────────────────────────────────────┐
56	        │  Layer 2: Shaper pack (workflow)  handoff)  ▼                                         │
57	        │  exec:pickup → breakdown → build → review → verify → prepare → finish → respond       │
58	        │  every box is also a standalone skill you can invoke alone                            │
59	        └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
60	```
61	
62	**The placement test** — for any piece of work, ask: *does this require drain-cycle to know what a role does (Layer 2 → pack), or only whether an artifact exists (Layer 1 → supervisor)?* That single question routes every ticket and prevents the layers from leaking into each other again.
63	
64	**Dual-mode falls out of the split.** Because Layer 2 is just skills and Layer 1 only reads artifacts, the skills carry no dependency on the supervisor. Invoked by hand they do the same thing; driven by a worker they do the same thing. drain-cycle is automation *on top of* the toolbox, never a precondition for it.
65	
66	## 4. Alternatives considered
67	
68	- **Do nothing** — keep the workflow inlined in `prompt.py` and duplicated in the pack. *Blast radius:* the two definitions drift (already happening — the prompt's `/shape:task` directive points at a superseded skill); the §7 failure class persists; no manual reuse of the workflow. *Reversal cost:* cheap today, rising with every divergence.
69	- **Fat supervisor** — drain-cycle inlines all role logic in Python. *Blast radius:* vendor lock-in (only a Claude worker can follow it), no by-hand reuse, every skill change becomes a Python change, prompt bloat. *Reversal cost:* high — unwinding role logic out of the orchestrator.
70	- **No supervisor** — skills self-supervise (each enforces its own caps/halt/resume). *Blast radius:* content-aware code doing process control; can't enforce cross-session token caps or resume after a crash, because a single skill can't see outside its own session. *Reversal cost:* high.
71	- **Recommended: two layers split on the artifact boundary** (§3), skills dual-mode, drain-cycle an optional supervisor. Beats the alternatives on every NFR in §2 and is the only one that delivers both use-modes from one definition.
72	
73	## 5. Recommended approach
74	
75	Adopt the §3 split as the invariant. Concretely:
76	
77	1. **Strip `prompt.py` to a pointer.** The worker prompt becomes "work issue X in worktree Y; follow `exec:pickup`" (≤ 15 lines, no procedure). The workflow lives once, in the pack.
78	2. **Keep the supervisor content-blind.** Its only decisions read artifacts: Done-state, `outcome_verdict`, `pr_urls`. Halting, reverting, resuming, recovering, grading — all keyed off those, never off the workflow's internal steps.
79	3. **Make every execution skill dual-mode by construction.** No skill assumes an orchestrator is present; the supervisor supplies only context (issue, worktree, base) and reads back artifacts.
80	4. **Treat the artifact contract as the API between repos.** Changes to run-log fields / handoff schema are versioned interface changes, reviewed as such.
81	
82	## 6. The verdict-crossing seam (the load-bearing unbuilt piece)
83	
84	The one genuinely new integration surface — and a likely one-way door, because it sets the handoff/run-log schema everything downstream reads.
85	
86	Layer 2 produces verdicts: `outcome_verdict` (from `exec:verify`), `prep_verdict` (from `pr-prepare`/`exec:finish`). Layer 1's enforcement and grading need them. But **today `handoff.py` carries only `pr_urls`** — there is no channel for a verdict to cross the boundary back into the run-log. Until there is, the supervisor can recover *unfinished* work (it can see "no `pr_urls`") but cannot act on *rejected* work (it can't see a FAIL verdict), and the grader can't enforce "every Done has a recorded verdict."
87	
88	This seam must be designed before the correctness work proceeds. The decision: **extend the handoff file as the single verdict-carrying artifact** the workflow writes and the supervisor reads — `{pr_urls, outcome_verdict, prep_verdict, ...}` — versus separate channels per verdict. Either way the schema is the contract; pick it deliberately (Open Question 1).
89	
90	## 7. Grounding incident
91	
92	Cycle `793fc9ea`, issue **ABA-383**: a worker pinned to `model:haiku` committed 4 reviewable slices, then exited *without* running review → `/shape:pr-finishing` → handoff → Done. The supervisor's not-Done halt reverted the issue and **stranded the committed work**. This is the architecture's motivating example: the worker did the Layer-2 work but skipped the finishing steps, and the Layer-1 supervisor — which should enforce completion from the outside — only knew how to halt, not recover. **ABA-393** (orchestrator-enforced completion: a sonnet finishing sub-agent recovers committed-but-unfinished work, opus for fixes) is the Layer-1 answer, and it is pure supervision: it reads "commits exist, but not Done / no `pr_urls`" and acts, never touching what the workflow means.
93	
94	## 8. How the projects map onto this design
95	
96	| Linear project | Layer | Status | What it is under this design |
97	|----------------|-------|--------|------------------------------|
98	| Issues drain end-to-end on first-party skills | L2 | Completed | Built the `exec:*` workflow skills — the reason L2 already exists |
99	| Consolidate Shaper's Lifecycle into Four Phase Skills | L2 | In progress | Consolidates the shaping-side skills (idea/project/design/delivery) |
100	| **drain-cycle supervises; the pack owns the workflow** | boundary | Planned | **Keystone — run first.** Strips `prompt.py` to a pointer, establishes the L1/L2 boundary |
101	| Multi-agent collaboration for correctness | L1 | Backlog | **Reframe** as the L1 enforcement+recording+grading project (see below) |
102	| Drain past the merge gate | L1 | idea | The supervisor's autonomy horizon: optimistic stacking → trust-tiered auto-merge → resident daemon. All Layer 1 (process), gated behind the boundary landing first |
103	
104	**Reframing "Multi-agent collaboration for correctness."** It predates the boundary, so its six-role *skill-building* is overtaken — those skills now exist in the pack. Split its tickets by the §3 placement test:
105	
106	- **Keep (Layer 1 — genuinely unbuilt supervision):** recording fields ABA-320/321/322; verifier-gated-Done enforcement ABA-328 + orchestrator recovery ABA-393 (two branches of one post-check — see §6/§7); grading ABA-333/334/336; the one missing Layer-2 skill, PR Responder + the `pr-feedback` subcommand ABA-332 (build the skill into the pack, the subcommand into drain-cycle).
107	- **Close or fold (overtaken — Layer-2 skill-build / worker-wiring that the boundary replaces):** ABA-324/326/327/330.
108	
109	Its KRs survive almost unchanged because they were already phrased as artifact checks ("every Done has a recorded `outcome_verdict`") — Layer-1 by construction. Only the implementation had drifted into the worker.
110	
111	## 9. Consequences
112	
113	- **The keystone lands first.** The boundary re-scope — strip `prompt.py`, point at `exec:pickup` — must land before anything else; it is the seam the whole design rests on, and it carries the real integration risk (a pointer-only prompt must drain a real ticket as well as the inlined one did). Everything else is additive once it holds.
114	- **Positive:** one definition serves both use-modes; a non-Claude worker can follow the same prose (vendor-agnostic); ABA-383-class recovery becomes natural Layer-1 behaviour; each layer is independently testable.
115	- **Negative:** the verdict-crossing seam is new surface and a schema one-way door (§6); two repos must stay in sync through the artifact contract, which is now a real interface to version.
116	
117	## 10. The artifacts that are the boundary also monitor it
118	
119	The design is observable through the same artifacts that *are* the boundary, so enforcement is mechanical, not manual:
120	
121	- **Metrics / logs:** the run-log entry per worker exit (exit state, `final_linear_state`, `halt_reason`, the verdict fields once §6 lands) and the OTel `drain.issue` span attributes.
122	- **Fitness functions:** the five NFR checks in §2 — boundary-purity grep, prompt-line cap, `drain-cycle grade` exit code, dual-mode self-trial, end-to-end autonomy scan. These are the alerts; a red fitness function is the signal the design has drifted.
123	- **Rollback:** the keystone re-scope has a defined fallback — if a pointer-only prompt causes 3 consecutive halted drains the inlined prompt would have completed, restore the inline tail and keep the re-scope to guardrails+logging only (the supervisor project's kill condition). ABA-393 recovery is bounded to one attempt per issue per run.
124	- **Failure modes:** worker skips finishing (→ L1 recovery, §7); verdict can't cross (→ §6 blocks correctness work, not the drain itself); restack conflict on merged upstream (→ halt-and-park, per *Drain past the merge gate*).
125	
126	## 11. Open questions
127	
128	1. **How does a verdict cross the boundary? (one-way door)** Does the handoff file become the single verdict-carrying artifact (`{pr_urls, outcome_verdict, prep_verdict, …}`), or do verdicts cross by separate channels? *Owner:* Anton. *Resolution gate:* the keystone re-scope design / the reframed recording milestone — blocks all Layer-1 correctness enforcement.
129	2. **Execution-skill verb namespace.** `exec:*` (current) vs `ship:*` (proposed in `lifecycle-expansion.md`). *Owner:* Anton. *Gate:* before the PR Responder skill is authored.
130	3. **Autonomy host.** Does end-to-end autonomy (Drain past the merge gate) run under a `/loop`/cron relaunch interim, or wait for the resident daemon? *Owner:* Anton. *Gate:* Gear-3 decision, after trust-tiered auto-merge ships.
131	4. **Where dual-mode parity is tested.** What fixture proves a skill behaves identically by hand and inside a worker? *Owner:* Anton. *Gate:* the execution project's fixture harness.
132	
```

=== style-rules.md ===
# Style Rules (all document types)

Apply these during Phase 4. The goal is the shortest path to the reader's mind without losing operational facts.

## Sentence mechanics

**Characters as subjects, actions as verbs.** Find the doer; make it the subject; make its activity a specific verb.

| Bad | Good |
|---|---|
| "There was a failure in the deployment process due to configuration drift." | "The deploy failed because the staging config drifted from prod." |
| "Request dropping occurs at high utilization." | "The load balancer drops requests above 1k RPS." |
| "It was decided that caching should be utilized." | "We will cache session tokens in Redis." |

**Old before new.** Start with what the reader knows; end with the new term. The end of a sentence is the stress position — the reader emphasizes whatever sits there.

| Bad | Good |
|---|---|
| "Edge Workers, which intercept requests at the CDN layer, will reduce load time." | "To cut load time, we will intercept requests at the CDN layer using Edge Workers." |

**Hand-product test.** Every task, goal, or milestone must imply a tangible end product the reader can visualize. "Investigate latency" fails; "a report naming the three slowest queries in /orders" passes.

## Nominalizations — reverse them

Abstract nouns derived from verbs freeze action. Turn them back into verbs.

| Frozen | Active |
|---|---|
| utilization | use |
| implementation | implement |
| optimization | optimize |
| investigation | investigate |
| facilitation | help / enable |
| determination | decide |
| reduction | reduce / cut |

## Vocabulary watchlist

Sweep the document for these and replace:

| Avoid | Prefer | Why |
|---|---|---|
| load-bearing / vital | essential / required | Overworked metaphor distracts from the technical reality |
| bottleneck | shortage / delay / hold-up | Cliché that hides the specific cause of constriction |
| target | objective / goal / aim | Often "attained" without being "hit" |
| blueprint | plan / scheme / spec | Implies a final design; usually misused for early ideas |
| ceiling | limit / maximum | "Raise the limit" is clearer than "raise the ceiling" |
| interface with | talk to | Jargon for a plain act |
| impact (as verb) | affect | "Impact" blunts a painful truth |
| leverage (as verb) | use | Same |
| basically / actually / quite / virtually / a bit | *(delete)* | Little qualifiers dilute authority |
| "at this point in time" | now | Shortest path to the mind |
| "in order to" | to | Same |
| "It should be noted that…" / "It is important to…" | *(start directly)* | Throat-clearing adds nothing |
| full and complete / each and every | full / each | Redundant pairs |

## The pruning safety rule

Concision serves clarity, never the reverse. Before deleting a sentence, ask: does it carry a dependency, risk, assumption, or constraint? If yes, compress it into fewer words — do not remove it. A short document that omits the rollback plan is worse than a longer one that keeps it.
```

thats better, i'd say also that the real thing i'm trying to do is to replace the work that I as a human normally do with an "orchestrator". in other words, the orchestrator is similar to the human layer with the goal that it will free me up to do higher-order activities rather than manual and fairly mechanical supervision. in saying that, one thing that i'm trading off is a feedback loop. when you supervise you can intervene and catch issues/problems with the workflow early on, you can also pivot and change direction rapidly. this is a deliberate trade off and i'm using it to drive work where i feel the trade off is worth it. ultimately i want to improve this incrementally over time so really good observability is a critical element to help me improve this product.

> AGENT

That's the heart of it — and it should be in the doc, not just in your head. The orchestrator *is* the human supervisory layer, automated; the deliberate cost is the live feedback loop; and observability is how you buy a delayed version of it back and improve the product. Let me weave those three in. First let me re-read the current state (it was reformatted).

124	3. **Autonomy host.** Does end-to-end autonomy (Drain past the merge gate) run under a `/loop`/cron relaunch interim, or wait for the resident daemon? *Owner:* Anton. *Gate:* Gear-3 decision, after trust-tiered auto-merge ships.
125	4. **Where dual-mode parity is tested.** What fixture proves a skill behaves identically by hand and inside a worker? *Owner:* Anton. *Gate:* the execution project's fixture harness.
126	
```

  "new_string": "The desired end-state: the **same skills** are the unit of work in both modes, and the automation is a thin layer that does what I do by hand as a supervisor — start the work, hold the guardrails, check the result. **The real goal is to replace my own mechanical supervision with an orchestrator** so my attention moves to higher-order work — scoping the next project, validating delivered work, improving the system — instead of watching each ticket run.\n\nThat autonomy comes at a price I take on deliberately: **automating supervision trades away the live feedback loop.** Supervising by hand, I catch a workflow going wrong early and I pivot fast; an orchestrated run gives that up — it executes to a halt or a Done and I inspect afterward. So I apply autonomy selectively, on work where the trade pays, and I lean on observability to buy back a delayed version of the loop — which is also how I improve the product over time."
}
```

113	
114	## 10. The artifacts that are the boundary also monitor it
115	
116	The design is observable through the same artifacts that *are* the boundary, so enforcement is mechanical, not manual:
117	
118	- **Metrics / logs:** the run-log entry per worker exit (exit state, `final_linear_state`, `halt_reason`, the verdict fields once §6 lands) and the OTel `drain.issue` span attributes.
119	- **Fitness functions:** the five NFR checks in §2 — boundary-purity grep, prompt-line cap, `drain-cycle grade` exit code, dual-mode self-trial, end-to-end autonomy scan. These are the alerts; a red fitness function is the signal the design has drifted.
120	- **Rollback:** the keystone re-scope has a defined fallback — if a pointer-only prompt causes 3 consecutive halted drains the inlined prompt would have completed, restore the inline tail and keep the re-scope to guardrails+logging only (the supervisor project's kill condition). ABA-393 recovery is bounded to one attempt per issue per run.
121	- **Failure modes:** worker skips finishing (→ L1 recovery, §7); verdict can't cross (→ §6 blocks correctness work, not the drain itself); restack conflict on merged upstream (→ halt-and-park, per *Drain past the merge gate*).
122	
```

105	
106	Its KRs survive almost unchanged because they were already phrased as artifact checks ("every Done has a recorded `outcome_verdict`") — Layer-1 by construction. Only the built code had drifted into the worker.
107	
108	## 9. Consequences
109	
110	- **The keystone lands first.** The boundary re-scope — strip `prompt.py`, point at `exec:pickup` — must land before anything else; it is the seam the whole design rests on, and it carries the real integration risk (a pointer-only prompt must drain a real ticket as well as the inlined one did). Everything else is additive once it holds.
111	- **Positive:** one definition serves both use-modes; a non-Claude worker can follow the same prose (vendor-agnostic); ABA-383-class recovery becomes natural Layer-1 behaviour; each layer is independently testable.
112	- **Negative:** the verdict-crossing seam is new surface and a schema one-way door (§6); two repos must stay in sync through the artifact contract, which is now a real interface to version.
113	
```

1	# AGENTS.md
2	
3	Instructions for any coding agent (Claude Code, Codex, etc.) working in this repository. Tool-agnostic by design — see `CLAUDE.md` for any Claude-Code-specific additions.
4	
5	## What this repo is
6	
7	`drain-cycle` is a CLI that picks up a well-scoped Linear cycle and executes its issues unattended, spawning a fresh `claude -p --dangerously-skip-permissions` session in an isolated worktree per issue. Python, single-user, personal product.
8	
9	The design rationale (decisions taken, alternatives considered, kill condition) lives in `docs/design-decisions.md`. Read it before making architectural changes.
10	
11	## Linear workflow
12	
13	Linear is authoritative for status. Local task lists are fine for within-session bookkeeping; they don't replace a Linear issue.
14	
15	**Project:** https://linear.app/ababushkin/project/autonomous-cycle-drain-eliminate-manual-shepherding-75daa8863063 (team ABA / Personal).

1	# drain-cycle
2	
3	Unattended execution of a Linear cycle. One invocation, no prompts; each issue runs in an isolated worktree under a fresh `claude -p` session that updates Linear itself.
4	
5	## Why this exists
6	
7	Today, executing a Linear cycle is manual: launch Claude per issue, watch it run, approve permissions, update Linear, repeat. Execution shepherding consumes time that should go to *scoping the next cycle* and *validating delivered work*. `drain-cycle` removes that shepherding so attention shifts back to scoping and validation. The full goal / KRs / kill condition live in the Linear project description.
8	
9	## Is this for you?
10	
11	Read this section before installing — `drain-cycle` is deliberately not for everyone.
12	
```

1	# Drain a project end-to-end on composable skills — supervised, not babysat
2	
3	**This is the guiding vision for the drain-cycle project.** It is not a one-off design doc: every decision, project, and ticket aligns to it, and every analytics review or retro is grounded in it. When a project and this doc disagree, change this doc first — deliberately — and let the projects follow. It consolidates the architecture content from `docs/initiative-multi-agent-collaboration.md` (the dispatcher framing) and `agent-skills-shaper/docs/ideas/lifecycle-expansion.md` (the process/workflow split); both are now facets of the single picture here.
4	
5	## 1. Problem
6	
7	I want one toolbox of capabilities that I can use two ways without rebuilding anything:
8	
9	- **By hand** — invoke a single discrete skill at the keyboard when I want exactly that one step (`/shape:design`, `/exec:review`, `/shape:pr-finishing`).
10	- **Unattended** — point an engine at a Linear project and have it run those same skills in the right sequence, ticket by ticket, to a merged reviewable PR — without me watching every step.
11	
12	Today both halves are broken. The skills exist but assume a human is driving; the automation (`drain-cycle`) re-implements the workflow as prompt prose instead of pointing at the skills; and the two have started to diverge. The failure that makes this concrete: a worker can finish the *work* and still not finish the *ticket*, and nothing catches it (§7).
13	
14	The desired end-state: the **same skills** are the unit of work in both modes, and the automation is a thin layer that does what I do by hand as a supervisor — start the work, hold the guardrails, check the result. **The real goal is to replace my own mechanical supervision with an orchestrator** so my attention moves to higher-order work — scoping the next project, validating delivered work, improving the system — instead of watching each ticket run.
15	
16	That autonomy comes at a price I take on deliberately: **automating supervision trades away the live feedback loop.** Supervising by hand, I catch a workflow going wrong early and I pivot fast; an orchestrated run gives that up — it executes to a halt or a Done and I inspect afterward. So I apply autonomy selectively, on work where the trade pays, and I lean on observability to buy back a delayed version of the loop — which is also how I improve the product over time.
17	
18	## 2. Context and constraints
19	
20	**Where this came from.** `drain-cycle` began as "drain a Linear cycle unattended — one ticket, one worker, one diff." Three threads then pulled it forward: the Shaper pack grew an execution half (the `exec:*` skills); a re-scope decision said the pack should *own* the workflow and drain-cycle should shrink to a supervisor (`lifecycle-expansion.md` §2); and a real incident (§7) showed the supervisor trusting the worker too much.
21	
22	**Prior decisions this inherits** (`docs/design-decisions.md`): §1 the spawned agent updates Linear itself; §10 headless workers inherit project config by symlink — *this is what makes a skill behave identically by hand and inside a worker*; §14 halted issues resume by reusing the worktree; §16–18 the Graphite stack-mode + handoff contract. Decision §17 ("`/shape:task` runs inside the worker session") is the high-water mark of the *old* inline-workflow approach and is superseded by the boundary below.
23	
24	**Non-functional requirements** (each with a fitness function — the automated check that keeps it true):
25	
26	| NFR                            | Target                                                                                                                             | Fitness function                                                              |
27	| ------------------------------ | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
28	| Supervisor carries no workflow | 0 workflow-procedure verbs (review/fix/commit/finish sequences) in `drain_cycle/prompt.py`; 0 `gh`/`gt` calls in `orchestrator.py` | `grep` check in the test suite                                                |
29	| Pointer-only prompt            | worker prompt template ≤ 15 lines                                                                                                  | `wc -l` on the template + procedure-verb grep (supervisor project KR3)        |
30	| Recording completeness         | 100% of verify-flow `Done` run-log entries carry an `outcome_verdict`                                                              | `drain-cycle grade` exits non-zero on any violation (correctness project KR2) |
31	| Dual-mode parity               | every execution skill runs to completion both invoked by hand and inside a worker, with no orchestrator-only assumption            | per-skill self-trial on both surfaces (execution project KR1)                 |
32	| End-to-end autonomy            | ≥ 4 of 5 hard tickets reach a merged PR with zero operator fix commits                                                             | `drain-cycle grade` + `git log` post-push-fix scan (correctness project KR1)  |
33	| Reconstructable without watching | every drained ticket answers "what happened and why" after the fact — exit state, halt reason, verdicts, cost, per-step trace — with no live observation needed | run-log entry + OTel `drain.issue` span resolve any ticket's run end-to-end   |
34	
35	## 3. The architecture: two layers, one artifact boundary
36	
37	"Orchestration" was doing two jobs under one word. Split them and the inconsistency dissolves.
38	
39	**Layer 2 — Workflow (the intra-issue layer). Owned by the Shaper pack.**
40	The content-aware sequence of roles inside one ticket: pickup → breakdown → build → review → verify → prepare PR → finish/submit → respond to feedback. It knows the order and the gates. It is already built: `exec:pickup` is the entry skill that delegates each step to its owning skill (`exec:breakdown` / `exec:build` / `exec:review` / `exec:verify` / `exec:finish`). Every step is *also* a skill you can invoke on its own — the sequence is a skill that calls skills, not a monolith.
41	
42	**Layer 1 — Supervision (the session layer). Owned by drain-cycle.**
43	This is the human supervisory role, automated — the mechanical part of what I do when I watch a drain by hand. The content-blind, vendor-agnostic process concerns: spawn a worker session, enforce guardrails (token/turn caps, timeouts), halt on breach, revert Linear state, resume across runs, recover stranded committed work, record the run-log, grade. It never knows what "review" or "verify" *means*. It only asks: *Is the issue Done? Is there an `outcome_verdict`? Does `.drain-handoff.json` carry `pr_urls`?*
44	
45	**The boundary is the artifact.** The two layers never call into each other — they communicate only through durable signals: **Linear state, run-log fields, and the `.drain-handoff.json` handoff file.** The workflow *writes* them; the supervisor *reads* them. That is the entire contract.
46	
47	```
48	                         ┌──────────────── Layer 1: drain-cycle (supervision) ─────────────────┐
49	   manual mode           │  spawn · guardrails · halt · revert · resume · recover · record ·   │
50	   (you, by hand) ─┐     │  grade        — reads artifacts only, never the workflow's steps    │
51	                   │     └───────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────┘
52	                   │            reads ▲   writes      │ spawns a worker that runs ▼
53	                   ▼                  │   (run-log,   │
54	        ┌─────────────────────────────│───Linear,────│────────────────────────────────────────┐
55	        │  Layer 2: Shaper pack (workflow)  handoff)  ▼                                         │
56	        │  exec:pickup → breakdown → build → review → verify → prepare → finish → respond       │
57	        │  every box is also a standalone skill you can invoke alone                            │
58	        └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
59	```
60	
61	**The placement test** — for any piece of work, ask: *does this require drain-cycle to know what a role does (Layer 2 → pack), or only whether an artifact exists (Layer 1 → supervisor)?* That single question routes every ticket and prevents the layers from leaking into each other again.
62	
63	**Dual-mode falls out of the split.** Because Layer 2 is just skills and Layer 1 only reads artifacts, the skills carry no dependency on the supervisor. Invoked by hand they do the same thing; driven by a worker they do the same thing. drain-cycle is automation *on top of* the toolbox, never a precondition for it.
64	
65	## 4. Alternatives considered
66	
67	- **Do nothing** — keep the workflow inlined in `prompt.py` and duplicated in the pack. *Blast radius:* the two definitions drift (already happening — the prompt's `/shape:task` directive points at a superseded skill); the §7 failure class persists; no manual reuse of the workflow. *Reversal cost:* cheap today, rising with every divergence.
68	- **Fat supervisor** — drain-cycle inlines all role logic in Python. *Blast radius:* vendor lock-in (only a Claude worker can follow it), no by-hand reuse, every skill change becomes a Python change, prompt bloat. *Reversal cost:* high — unwinding role logic out of the orchestrator.
69	- **No supervisor** — skills self-supervise (each enforces its own caps/halt/resume). *Blast radius:* content-aware code doing process control; can't enforce cross-session token caps or resume after a crash, because a single skill can't see outside its own session. *Reversal cost:* high.
70	- **Recommended: two layers split on the artifact boundary** (§3), skills dual-mode, drain-cycle an optional supervisor. Beats the alternatives on every NFR in §2 and is the only one that delivers both use-modes from one definition.
71	
72	## 5. Recommended approach
73	
74	Adopt the §3 split as the invariant. Concretely:
75	
76	1. **Strip `prompt.py` to a pointer.** The worker prompt becomes "work issue X in worktree Y; follow `exec:pickup`" (≤ 15 lines, no procedure). The workflow lives once, in the pack.
77	2. **Keep the supervisor content-blind.** Its only decisions read artifacts: Done-state, `outcome_verdict`, `pr_urls`. Halting, reverting, resuming, recovering, grading — all keyed off those, never off the workflow's internal steps.
78	3. **Make every execution skill dual-mode by construction.** No skill assumes an orchestrator is present; the supervisor supplies only context (issue, worktree, base) and reads back artifacts.
79	4. **Treat the artifact contract as the API between repos.** Changes to run-log fields / handoff schema are versioned interface changes, reviewed as such.
80	
81	## 6. The verdict-crossing seam (the one piece still unbuilt)
82	
83	The one new integration surface — and a likely one-way door, because it sets the handoff/run-log schema everything downstream reads.
84	
85	Layer 2 produces verdicts: `outcome_verdict` (from `exec:verify`), `prep_verdict` (from `pr-prepare`/`exec:finish`). Layer 1's enforcement and grading need them. But **today `handoff.py` carries only `pr_urls`** — there is no channel for a verdict to cross the boundary back into the run-log. Until there is, the supervisor can recover *unfinished* work (it can see "no `pr_urls`") but cannot act on *rejected* work (it can't see a FAIL verdict), and the grader can't enforce "every Done has a recorded verdict."
86	
87	This seam must be designed before the correctness work proceeds. The decision: **extend the handoff file as the single verdict-carrying artifact** the workflow writes and the supervisor reads — `{pr_urls, outcome_verdict, prep_verdict, ...}` — versus separate channels per verdict. Either way the schema is the contract; pick it deliberately (Open Question 1).
88	
89	## 7. The incident that grounds this: work finished, ticket stranded
90	
91	Cycle `793fc9ea`, issue **ABA-383**: a worker pinned to `model:haiku` committed 4 reviewable slices, then exited *without* running review → `/shape:pr-finishing` → handoff → Done. The supervisor's not-Done halt reverted the issue and **stranded the committed work**. This is the architecture's motivating example: the worker did the Layer-2 work but skipped the finishing steps, and the Layer-1 supervisor — which should enforce completion from the outside — only knew how to halt, not recover. **ABA-393** (orchestrator-enforced completion: a sonnet finishing sub-agent recovers committed-but-unfinished work, opus for fixes) is the Layer-1 answer, and it is pure supervision: it reads "commits exist, but not Done / no `pr_urls`" and acts, never touching what the workflow means.
92	
93	## 8. How the projects map onto this design
94	
95	| Linear project                                         | Layer    | Status      | What it is under this design                                                                                                                                       |
96	| ------------------------------------------------------ | -------- | ----------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
97	| Issues drain end-to-end on first-party skills          | L2       | Completed   | Built the `exec:*` workflow skills — the reason L2 already exists                                                                                                  |
98	| Consolidate Shaper's Lifecycle into Four Phase Skills  | L2       | In progress | Consolidates the shaping-side skills (idea/project/design/delivery)                                                                                                |
99	| **drain-cycle supervises; the pack owns the workflow** | boundary | Planned     | **Keystone — run first.** Strips `prompt.py` to a pointer, establishes the L1/L2 boundary                                                                          |
100	| Multi-agent collaboration for correctness              | L1       | Backlog     | **Reframe** as the L1 enforcement+recording+grading project (see below)                                                                                            |
101	| Drain past the merge gate                              | L1       | idea        | The supervisor's autonomy horizon: optimistic stacking → trust-tiered auto-merge → resident daemon. All Layer 1 (process), gated behind the boundary landing first |
102	
103	**Reframing "Multi-agent collaboration for correctness."** It predates the boundary, so its six-role *skill-building* is overtaken — those skills now exist in the pack. Split its tickets by the §3 placement test:
104	
105	- **Keep (Layer 1 — supervision not yet built):** recording fields ABA-320/321/322; verifier-gated-Done enforcement ABA-328 + orchestrator recovery ABA-393 (two branches of one post-check — see §6/§7); grading ABA-333/334/336; the one missing Layer-2 skill, PR Responder + the `pr-feedback` subcommand ABA-332 (build the skill into the pack, the subcommand into drain-cycle).
106	- **Close or fold (overtaken — Layer-2 skill-build / worker-wiring that the boundary replaces):** ABA-324/326/327/330.
107	
108	Its KRs survive almost unchanged because they were already phrased as artifact checks ("every Done has a recorded `outcome_verdict`") — Layer-1 by construction. Only the built code had drifted into the worker.
109	
110	## 9. Consequences
111	
112	- **The keystone lands first.** The boundary re-scope — strip `prompt.py`, point at `exec:pickup` — must land before anything else; it is the seam the whole design rests on, and it carries the real integration risk (a pointer-only prompt must drain a real ticket as well as the inlined one did). Everything else is additive once it holds.
113	- **Positive:** one definition serves both use-modes; a non-Claude worker can follow the same prose (vendor-agnostic); ABA-383-class recovery becomes natural Layer-1 behaviour; each layer is independently testable.
114	- **Negative:** the verdict-crossing seam is new surface and a schema one-way door (§6); two repos must stay in sync through the artifact contract, which is now a real interface to version.
115	- **The trade-off is the feedback loop, by design.** Handing supervision to the orchestrator removes the live intervention and rapid pivot that watching a drain by hand gives me. I accept this selectively — on work scoped well enough that the trade pays — and it makes observability essential rather than optional: once I step out of the loop, the run-log, traces, and grade *are* my feedback, delayed and batched (§10). The improvement path is to widen the set of work where the trade pays, which depends directly on how good that observability gets.
116	
117	## 10. Observability is the feedback loop I traded away, bought back
118	
119	Once I step out of the live loop (§9), observability stops being mere operability — it becomes the delayed feedback loop that tells me where a drain went wrong and, run over run, where the product has to improve. It rides the same artifacts that *are* the boundary, so enforcement and inspection are mechanical, not manual. Making it good is what widens the set of work I can safely hand off, so it is a first-class deliverable, not an afterthought:
120	
121	- **Metrics / logs:** the run-log entry per worker exit (exit state, `final_linear_state`, `halt_reason`, the verdict fields once §6 lands) and the OTel `drain.issue` span attributes.
122	- **Fitness functions:** the five NFR checks in §2 — boundary-purity grep, prompt-line cap, `drain-cycle grade` exit code, dual-mode self-trial, end-to-end autonomy scan. These are the alerts; a red fitness function is the signal the design has drifted.
123	- **Rollback:** the keystone re-scope has a defined fallback — if a pointer-only prompt causes 3 consecutive halted drains the inlined prompt would have completed, restore the inline tail and keep the re-scope to guardrails+logging only (the supervisor project's kill condition). ABA-393 recovery is bounded to one attempt per issue per run.
124	- **Failure modes:** worker skips finishing (→ L1 recovery, §7); verdict can't cross (→ §6 blocks correctness work, not the drain itself); restack conflict on merged upstream (→ halt-and-park, per *Drain past the merge gate*).
125	
126	## 11. Open questions
127	
128	1. **How does a verdict cross the boundary? (one-way door)** Does the handoff file become the single verdict-carrying artifact (`{pr_urls, outcome_verdict, prep_verdict, …}`), or do verdicts cross by separate channels? *Owner:* Anton. *Resolution gate:* the keystone re-scope design / the reframed recording milestone — blocks all Layer-1 correctness enforcement.
129	2. **Execution-skill verb namespace.** `exec:*` (current) vs `ship:*` (proposed in `lifecycle-expansion.md`). *Owner:* Anton. *Gate:* before the PR Responder skill is authored.
130	3. **Autonomy host.** Does end-to-end autonomy (Drain past the merge gate) run under a `/loop`/cron relaunch interim, or wait for the resident daemon? *Owner:* Anton. *Gate:* Gear-3 decision, after trust-tiered auto-merge ships.
131	4. **Where dual-mode parity is tested.** What fixture proves a skill behaves identically by hand and inside a worker? *Owner:* Anton. *Gate:* the execution project's fixture harness.
132	
```
