1	# Linear initiative — multi-agent collaboration for correctness
2	
3	> **Now a facet of the north-star.** The system architecture this initiative assumed is consolidated in [`docs/designs/end-to-end-skill-drain.md`](designs/end-to-end-skill-drain.md). Under that design this initiative is reframed as the **Layer-1 (supervision) enforcement + recording + grading** project — its six-role *skill-building* is overtaken by the pack's `exec:*` skills. Read the north-star first; treat the role/flow detail below as historical context for the recording/enforcement/grading work that survives.
4	
5	**Six agent-driven roles take a Linear ticket from intake to merged PR — so hard tickets don't silently ship with missing AC.**
6	
7	**The bet.** drain-cycle today drains a Linear cycle unattended — one ticket, one worker, one diff. The next step: six agent-driven roles operating end-to-end on the same ticket — task-shaping before code, outcome verification before Done, structured PR preparation, and autonomous handling of human review feedback. Same operator, same tickets, less manual shepherding.
8	
9	**What we'll know in 5 hard tickets.** Of the next 5 hard tickets drained under the new flow, the bet is ≥ 3 ship and merge with no operator-pushed fix commits. Otherwise the kill condition triggers (§5) and contract enforcement reverts.
10	
11	---
12	
13	## 1 The problem and the bet
14	
15	**Why this matters beyond drain-cycle.** drain-cycle is the batch driver for a growing toolbox of composable skills and MCPs. Each new skill compounds across both modes of use — at the keyboard during interactive work, and inside a worker session during a drain. This initiative ships the next four skills plus two CLI subcommands the toolbox needs to take a hard Linear ticket from intake to a merged PR, plus the durable contracts (run-log fields, Linear-issue dual-write, OTel attrs) that make their outputs inspectable afterwards.
16	
17	drain-cycle evolves from "drain a Linear cycle unattended" toward a **UNIX-philosophy dispatcher for composable skill primitives** — Linear tickets as the work queue, skills+MCPs as the small tools, and `drain-cycle` itself as the batch-mode shell that composes them. The operator (you) drives the *why* (initiative goal + KRs) and the *what* (outcome-focused Linear issue); agents handle the *how* through a defined set of skills. The same skills run manually at the keyboard or automatically inside a worker session — design decision §10's symlink makes this true.
18	
19	The operator failure mode this initiative addresses: **complex tickets that can't be sliced further** where the worker forgets details, under-verifies, or submits an unstructured PR — followed by human review comments piling up while the operator manually re-invokes the agent each round. Three concrete failures today:
20	
21	1. **Silent Done on hard tickets.** Complex unsliceable tickets get drained; the worker self-asserts Done; the diff misses AC; the operator finds out at PR review and fixes by hand. *(e.g., a ticket asks for "halt the drain if Linear label resolution returns ambiguous matches"; the worker ships the label-parse change but never adds the halt logic; the diff passes self-review because nothing it touches asserts the halt-on-ambiguity contract.)* Left unaddressed, the unattended-drain promise breaks on the tickets where it matters most.
22	2. **Unstructured PR submission.** PRs land without What/Why/Focus guidance; the reviewer has to guess what to look at, or the operator manually rewrites the body. *(e.g., a multi-file refactor lands with a subject line and no body; the reviewer reads the diff blind to find out whether the risky bit is in `labels.py` or `worker.py`.)* Left unaddressed, PR review burden stays entirely on the human — auto-mergeable changes get the same scrutiny as risky ones, and review throughput suffers.
23	3. **Stalled feedback loops.** Human review comments accumulate on stacked PRs; the operator manually re-invokes claude each round. *(e.g., a stacked PR gets three rounds of review comments across two days; each round the operator opens the worktree, re-launches claude, and shepherds the fix manually.)* Left unaddressed, the agent ecosystem stops at code-submission time instead of running end-to-end through merge.
24	
25	The bet: six agent-driven roles end-to-end, each backed by a skill (or CLI subcommand) fitting the UNIX-philosophy frame. A drained ticket flows through the pipeline below; the per-role detail table follows.
26	
27	```
28	┌─ Operator (you) ──────────────────────────────────────────┐
29	│  throughout · defines why (KRs) + what (Linear ticket)    │
30	└──────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────┘
31	                               ▼
32	┌─ Task Shaper ─────────────────────────────────────────────┐
33	│  before code · /shape:task                                │
34	│  → enriched AC + sizing decision + per-stack task list    │
35	└──────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────┘
36	                               ▼
37	┌─ Implementer ─────────────────────────────────────────────┐
38	│  middle · 6 existing build/test/review/commit skills      │
39	│  → code + tests + in-session code review                  │
40	└──────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────┘
41	                               ▼
42	┌─ Outcome Verifier ────────────────────────────────────────┐
43	│  after impl · /shape:verify-implementation                │
44	│  → pass/fail verdict in run log (gates Done)              │
45	└──────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────┘
46	                               ▼
47	┌─ PR Preparer ─────────────────────────────────────────────┐
48	│  before submit · /shape:pr-prepare                        │
49	│  → What/Why/Focus body OR auto-merge label                │
50	└──────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────┘
51	                               ▼
52	┌─ PR Responder ────────────────────────────────────────────┐
53	│  on review feedback · /shape:pr-respond                   │
54	│  (operator launches via `drain-cycle pr-feedback`)        │
55	│  → fix lands on stack · comment IDs tracked               │
56	└──────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────┘
57	                               ▼
58	┌─ Grader ──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
59	│  post-drain · `drain-cycle grade-draft <issue>`           │
60	│  → ~/.drain-cycle/grades/<issue>.md                       │
61	│    (status: draft → operator confirms)                    │
62	└───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
63	```
64	
65	| # | Role | Phase | Skill / CLI | Status |
66	|---|---|---|---|---|
67	| 0 | **Operator** (you) | Throughout | Operator-side skill table; defines *why* + *what* | — |
68	| 1 | **Task Shaper** | Before code | `/shape:task` *(complements `/shape:planning-and-task-breakdown`)* | [R] |
69	| 2 | **Implementer** | Middle | 6 existing skills (incremental-implementation, test-driven-development, source-driven-development, code-review-and-quality, git-workflow-and-versioning, debugging-and-error-recovery) | [E] |
70	| 3 | **Outcome Verifier** | After implementation, before Done | `/shape:verify-implementation` *(new)* | [N] |
71	| 4 | **PR Preparer** | After Outcome Verifier passes, before PR submission | `/shape:pr-prepare` *(new)* | [N] |
72	| 5 | **PR Responder** | On new human review comments (operator-launched polling loop) | `/shape:pr-respond` *(new)*, launched via `drain-cycle pr-feedback` subcommand | [N] |
73	| 6 | **Grader** (post-drain) | After worker session finishes | `drain-cycle grade-draft <issue>` CLI subcommand | [—] |
74	
75	Roles 1 and 3 are a **complementary pair**: `/shape:task` defines the outcome-shaped task list at the start; `/shape:verify-implementation` validates the outcome at the end. Roles 4 and 5 are the **PR-side pair**: Preparer shapes outbound PRs, Responder addresses inbound review feedback. Role 6 is operator-confirm scaffolding for grading the initiative against its KRs.
76	
77	**If the initiative is built:**
78	
79	- **Hard tickets clear AC before they land.** The Outcome Verifier sees the diff before Done is recorded; the verdict goes to the run log; halt-on-fail leaves the worktree intact for operator inspection rather than producing a silent partial.
80	- **PR review starts with a structured map.** Reviewers open a PR and see What / Why / Focus from the start; auto-mergeable changes route past human review correctly; risky changes get the human attention they earn. Review throughput rises because reviewers no longer parse intent from the diff alone.
81	- **Human review feedback closes its own loop.** The operator launches `drain-cycle pr-feedback`; new review comments get addressed on the right stack node; comment IDs are tracked to prevent double-handling; the loop continues until the operator stops it. The agent ecosystem runs end-to-end through merge rather than stopping at submit-time.
82	
83	**Constraints:**
84	
85	- Subscription auth only (Claude Code / Codex CLI subprocess) — no API-key models. GitHub webhooks are free, but the agent can't run inside GitHub Actions; agent work happens on the operator's machine.
86	- Correctness > throughput. No parallel drains. No throughput-focused changes.
87	
88	## 2 Affected repos
89	
90	- **`drain-cycle`** — primary. Worker integration of roles 1–5, run-log schema extensions, `verify` label mechanism, CLI subcommands (`grade-draft`, `grade --flow=verify`), supersession of design decision §1, OTel attribute additions.
91	- **`agent-skills-shaper`** — secondary. Receives one per-ticket task-shaping skill (`/shape:task`, complementing the whole-project `/shape:planning-and-task-breakdown`) and three new skills (`/shape:verify-implementation`, `/shape:pr-prepare`, `/shape:pr-respond`).
92	
93	## 3 Goal and Key results
94	
95	The KRs are plain English — a non-pack-author should grade each in 30 seconds.
96	
97	### 3.0 Goal
98	
99	**For** the operator running drain-cycle on outcome-focused Linear tickets, **we want** six agent-driven roles that take work from ticket to merged PR — task-shaping before code, outcome verification before Done, structured PR preparation, and autonomous response to human review comments — **so that** complex tickets don't silently land with missing AC, PRs are reviewable without guesswork, and human review feedback doesn't create a manual re-invocation tax.
100	
101	### 3.1 KR1 (stretch) — Of the next 5 hard tickets drained with the new flow, ≥ 3 ship and merge with no operator-pushed fix commits *(bet)*
102	
103	- **plain-English version:** when I run drain-cycle on a hard ticket I couldn't break into smaller pieces, six agent roles take it from intake to merged PR. The bet: this catches enough real problems and structures the PRs well enough that most merge cleanly without me pushing fix commits.
104	- **baseline:** 0 of 0 — none of the six roles exist as a connected flow today.
105	- **target:** ≥ 3 of the next 5 hard tickets drained with the new flow ship and merge with no operator-pushed fix commits.
106	- **measured over:** the first 5 hard tickets drained with the new flow (~2 cycles at current cadence).
107	- **how we'll grade it:** per-ticket grade markdown at `~/.drain-cycle/grades/<issue>.md` (auto-drafted by `drain-cycle grade-draft <issue>`, operator confirms `draft` → `confirmed`); `drain-cycle grade --flow=verify` reports the rolled-up pass-rate across the window.
108	
109	### 3.2 KR2 (commit) — Every ticket reaching Done in Linear under the new flow has a recorded Outcome Verifier verdict in the run log; zero exceptions *(brake)*
110	
111	- **plain-English version:** the worker no longer marks its own ticket Done. The contract: a ticket using the new flow reaches Done only if the run log shows the Outcome Verifier ran and produced a verdict. Protects against the failure mode where the agent thinks it's finished but missed half the AC.
112	- **baseline:** silent self-assertion currently possible (per design decision §1); the count of past silent Dones is unknown because the recording fields don't exist yet.
113	- **target:** 0 tickets reach Done in Linear under the new flow without an `outcome_verdict` recorded in the run log.
114	- **measured over:** every cycle running the new flow, for the initiative's duration (cap at 10 cycles if the initiative runs long).
115	- **how we'll grade it:** `drain-cycle grade` fails any entry where `flow == "verify"` AND `final_linear_state == "completed"` AND `outcome_verdict` is absent. Exit code ≠ 0 on any violation. One violation in any cycle fails the KR — this is a brake, not a rate.
116	
117	### 3.3 KR3 (commit) — Every PR submitted under the new flow has the structured What / Why / Focus body, or is correctly routed to auto-merge *(brake)*
118	
119	- **plain-English version:** the PR Preparer either writes a structured What / Why / Focus body and routes the PR for human review, or correctly identifies the PR as auto-mergeable (no critical focus items) and routes it to auto-merge. Either way, every PR ships with a documented routing decision and a reviewable body.
120	- **baseline:** today, PRs from drained tickets carry whatever body the worker produced — typically a one-line subject and no review guidance.
121	- **target:** 0 PRs submitted under the new flow without either (a) structured What/Why/Focus body OR (b) auto-merge label with PR Preparer reasoning attached.
122	- **measured over:** every PR submitted under the new flow, for the initiative's duration.
123	- **how we'll grade it:** PR Preparer writes its decision to a `prep_verdict` field on the run-log entry; `drain-cycle grade` fails any verify-flow entry where `prep_verdict` is absent or where the verdict says "structured body" but the PR body is empty or single-line.
124	
125	The PR Responder (role 5) gets no KR of its own — KR1 captures it structurally (clean-merge rate degrades if the responder doesn't address review feedback).
126	
127	## 4 Appetite
128	
129	**~18–22 issues across five milestones.** Each milestone ends with a review gate — the operator reviews progress and either continues to the next milestone or pauses for a reshape/kill decision if something clear has surfaced. The gate default is review-and-continue, not stop-the-line. Whole-initiative kill conditions are separate (§5).
130	
131	### M1 — Recording infrastructure
132	
133	Build the data layer first, behaviour later.
134	
135	- `verify` Linear label parser added alongside `repo:` / `model:` resolution in `labels.py`.
136	- Run-log schema fields: `flow` (string, e.g. `"verify"`, or `null` for default flow), `outcome_verdict` (object: `{result, findings[], invoked_at}` or `null`), `prep_verdict` (object: `{result, route, reasoning}` or `null`), `responder_runs[]` (array of objects: `{comment_ids[], invoked_at, result}` — appended each time the responder runs).
137	- Matching attributes on the `drain.issue` OTel span.
138	- `docs/design-decisions.md` §1 supersession: write the successor decision explaining the verifier-gated Done contract; mark §1 as superseded.
139	
140	**Gate:** an end-to-end `verify`-labelled ticket drains; new fields populate (all `null` since no roles are wired yet); existing behaviour unchanged.
141	
142	### M2 — Worker-internal loop (Task Shaper, Outcome Verifier, halt path)
143	
144	- **Task Shaper** (`/shape:task`): per-ticket task-shaping skill that takes a Linear issue and produces an enriched AC checklist + sizing decision (single stack vs N stacks) + per-stack task list. Distinct from `/shape:planning-and-task-breakdown` (whole-project / design-doc shaping); the two coexist in the toolbox at different scopes. The precise interface is resolved in design decision §17 (`docs/design-decisions.md`): `/shape:task` runs inside the worker session at implementation time; `/shape:planning-and-task-breakdown` stays at design-doc scope and is not extended to emit per-ticket artefacts.
145	- **Where the sizing decision lands**: dual-write. (a) Fed into the implementer in-session as context; (b) appended to the **Linear issue body** so future resumes / pickups carry the full context without re-running the Task Shaper. The Linear write is the durable artefact.
146	- **Implementer integration**: worker session pulls the Task Shaper output as context; no other implementer changes.
147	- **Outcome Verifier** (`/shape:verify-implementation`): new skill. Inputs: ticket body + Task Shaper output + diff + run-log. Output: structured verdict (pass / fail + findings) written to `outcome_verdict`.
148	- **Halt-on-fail path**: extends decision §8 / §9 machinery — on Outcome Verifier fail, leave worktree, halt cycle, name the breach in `cycle_halt_reason`.
149	
150	**Gate:** 3 verify-labelled hard tickets drained; verdicts recorded; halt path exercised at least once on a deliberate failure (e.g. a synthetic ticket whose AC the implementer can't satisfy).
151	
152	### M3 — PR-shape gate (PR Preparer)
153	
154	- **PR Preparer** (`/shape:pr-prepare`): new skill. Reads stack PRs via Graphite MCP; writes What / Why / Focus body on each; decides auto-merge vs human-review per the carve-out rule (small + no human review needed → auto-merge; otherwise → human-review).
155	- **Auto-merge mechanism**: Graphite-native (`gt submit --merge-when-ready`-equivalent).
156	- Records the decision to `prep_verdict` on the run-log entry.
157	
158	**Gate:** 3 stacks emitted, all with structured What / Why / Focus bodies or auto-merge labels. PR-body assertion in `drain-cycle grade` passes on all three.
159	
160	### M4 — PR-feedback loop (PR Responder)
161	
162	- **PR Responder** (`/shape:pr-respond`): new skill. Reads new review comments on stack PRs via Graphite MCP; makes the fix in the right worktree; pushes to the stack; appends to `responder_runs[]`.
163	- **Trigger model**: a separate operator-launched subcommand — `drain-cycle pr-feedback`. The operator launches it explicitly; the command then runs as a polling loop, addressing PR comments as they arrive, until the operator stops it. Not invoked implicitly at drain-start, not a GitHub Action, not a boot-time background daemon. A foreground polling agent whose lifetime the operator owns.
164	- **Idempotency**: each comment ID tracked in `responder_runs[].comment_ids[]` so the same comment isn't addressed twice across `pr-feedback` invocations.
165	
166	**Gate:** one real PR with operator-left review feedback gets addressed end-to-end by the responder; the fix lands on the stack; the run-log shows the responder run.
167	
168	### M5 — Grading + cost + close-out
169	
170	- **Per-ticket grade template** at `~/.drain-cycle/grades/<issue>.md` with checklist + `status:` field.
171	- **`drain-cycle grade-draft <issue>` CLI subcommand**: one-shot grading prompt. Runs automatically on drain completion (writes `status: draft`); also runs manually for retro-fill on past tickets.
172	- **`drain-cycle grade --flow=verify` subcommand**: reads `confirmed` grade files, reports pass-rate across a ticket window, warns about un-confirmed drafts.
173	- **Silent-Done detection** (KR2 grader): exit nonzero on any `flow == "verify"` AND `final_linear_state == "completed"` AND `outcome_verdict == null` entry.
174	- **Cost-impact handling**: measure per-ticket token multiplier under the new flow; add `verify`-flow-specific overrides in `limits.yml` if cycle cap pressure surfaces.
175	- **Post-launch review**: write a Linear comment on each KR's status at the end of the initiative window; either ship cleanly, pause, or kill.
176	
177	**Gate:** KR grading runs end-to-end; one of {ship cleanly, pause, kill} is decided and recorded.
178	
179	## 5 Kill condition
180	
181	**Primary kill (whole-initiative):** after running the new flow on 5+ hard tickets across 2 cycles, the Outcome Verifier produces zero true-positive findings (every halt was a false positive, and verifier-passed Dones don't visibly improve PR-review outcomes vs default-flow drains).
182	
183	**Secondary concerns (watched at each milestone's review gate, not auto-kills):**
184	
185	- M1 — schema additions break run-log readers or downstream tooling.
186	- M3 — PR Preparer's bodies don't measurably reduce reviewer cognitive load (signal: the operator subjectively prefers the raw worker-produced PRs).
187	- M4 — PR Responder either (a) requires operator intervention to address comments cleanly >50% of the time, or (b) drops or misses comments in practice.
188	
189	Any of these surfacing at a milestone review prompts a discussion (continue / reshape / kill), but the default is continue. Only the primary kill above is automatic.
190	
191	**Action on kill:** revert the contract enforcement (the `verify` label becomes a no-op or is removed); keep the recording fields — they stay in run log + OTel because they're cheap and useful for future investigation; record the negative result as a new decision in `docs/design-decisions.md`.
192	
193	## 6 Out of scope (explicitly)
194	
195	- **Parallel drains.** Decision §8's serial-drain stance unchanged.
196	- **Replacing Graphite.** The PR-side work assumes the Graphite stack flow; switching PR-management tools is a separate decision.
197	- **Multi-operator / multi-tenant workflows.** Single-user product per `AGENTS.md` ("Python, single-user, personal product").
198	
```

(a) The master design-rationale doc (17 decisions recorded), explicitly anchored to the project vision (`docs/designs/end-to-end-skill-drain.md` — the two-layer supervisor/workflow architecture). No explicit date; cross-references through 2026-05-22 (US-B smoke, ABA-300 spike), latest decision (§17) on `/shape:task` invocation dated implicitly to the multi-agent-initiative (Task 3 / ABA-220 timeframe, mid-2026). Opens with "ADRs would be heavier than this tool needs."
(b) **Explicitly frames the two-layer architecture as the guiding vision** (opening paragraph); §1–8 cover base orchestrator (US-A through D era), §9–16 cover resource guardrails and resumption, §17 covers the multi-agent `/shape:task` invocation interface (initiative Task 3). This is the authoritative architecture doc.
(c) **Not superseded**; it is the *lived* design rationale. Individual older decisions are **superseded *within the doc itself***: §1 is superseded-by-§N (per Task 2 of multi-agent-collaboration), §14 supersedes the `rerun-after-halt-detect-cleanly.md` task plan.
(d) No staleness markers except deliberate supersession notes (§14 on §1, rerun plan on task doc). Up-to-date through the latest initiative tasks.

**Section headings in design-decisions.md (top ~8 headings visible)**:
1. The spawned agent updates Linear itself
2. `--dangerously-skip-permissions` is accepted
3. Fresh worktree per issue, not a shared workspace
4. Run-log is one file per invocation, not one file per cycle
5. Each issue declares its target repo via a `repo:<name>` Linear label
6. Installed as a `uv tool`, with the secret read from `~/.drain-cycle/.env`
7. Workers default to Sonnet; a `model:` label overrides per issue
8. Workers use stream-json output; usage is parsed from the wire and the worker leads its own process group
[... decisions 9–17 visible in the longer read ...]

## Integration-Test Files Comparison

**docs/adrs/0001-integration-test-architecture.md** vs. **docs/integration-test-architecture.md**:

**Does one supersede the other?** No — they are complementary, not superseding.

- **0001 (ADR)**: The *decision artifact* — chosen approach (parent HTTP mock + MCP shim + real claude + in-process harness), rejected alternatives (scripted claude, hybrid, function-injection, VCR), and revisit conditions. ~45 lines, decision-focused.
- **integration-test-architecture.md** (design doc): The *full design artifact* — context, system context diagram, workflow, three pillars detailed, cost/runtime/determinism strategy, per-pillar specifications, production code changes required, test-infra correctness checks, failure-mode coverage matrix, scenario catalogue (initial and target), slices (12 planned), and critical files. ~399 lines, comprehensive.

The ADR text explicitly states (line 8): "See `docs/integration-test-architecture.md` for the full design." The design doc reciprocates (line 108): "The architectural decision … is recorded in [`docs/adrs/0001-integration-test-architecture.md`]". The ADR is the *decision slice* of the design; the design doc is the full spec that the decision enables. They co-exist for different purposes: the ADR for historical traceability of the chosen approach, the design doc for implementation guidance. **Neither supersedes the other.**
```

```
1	# Drain past the merge gate
2	
3	## Problem Statement
4	How might we let a drain run keep doing useful work — and keep turning hands-free — when the only thing left blocking it is a human review-and-merge gate on PRs it already opened?
5	
6	## Context
7	drain-cycle is transactional per issue: a worker marks a Linear issue **Done**, the orchestrator stacks its PR via Graphite, and continues. Within a run, downstream work stacks fine. The pain appears when the drain runs out of *runnable* work: anything still blocked by an **unmerged** dependency gets **deferred** (`linear.py`, external-blocker defer — `state_type not in _RESOLVED_STATE_TYPES`). The run ends, and the cycle lingers until the owner reviews + merges. The human merge serializes everything downstream.
8	
9	Desired end-state (from session): build on unmerged work, auto-merge *trusted* classes, and a loop that resumes as merges land — minimal owner touch points. Default trust posture is conservative (gate unless told otherwise).
10	
11	## Recommended Direction
12	A **three-gear** evolution, each independently shippable, smallest-blast-radius first. **The destination is a real resident daemon** — a drain-cycle process that stays alive, watches its own PRs, and will eventually host a full PR-review lifecycle (request review → ingest feedback → revise → re-submit → merge) as a long-running loop. The gears below are ordered so each one ships value on its own *and* lays a brick toward that daemon; a cron/`/loop` relaunch is an acceptable **interim** host for Gears 1–2 while the resident process is built, not the end state.
```

6	
7	## Context
8	
9	Today `drain-cycle` only tests against a real Linear cycle. ok now lets review the vision against the writing guidelines below. they're similar to /writing-refinement : 

1. Structure via the "S-C-Q" Framework
A vision statement is most engaging when it resolves a specific tension
. Barbara Minto suggests using a narrative flow to establish relevance before presenting your "Answer" (the vision)
.
Situation: Begin with a non-controversial statement about the current state of your field or product area that your audience already knows or will accept as true
.
Complication: Describe what has changed or what problem has arisen in that situation (e.g., a technological shift, a lagging performance, or an unmet customer need)
.
Question: This complication should naturally trigger a question in the reader's mind (e.g., "How can we adapt?" or "What should our strategy be?")
.
Answer (The Vision): Your vision statement serves as the resolution to this story
.
2. Motivate through "Practical Problems"
A compelling vision must address a problem your audience actually cares about
.
Define the Cost: Joseph Williams and Joseph Bizup emphasize that a problem is only a problem if it has a cost—a palpable consequence like lost time, money, or happiness
. Your vision is the "solution" to this cost
.
Focus on Themes: Put your most important concepts in the "stress position" (at the end) of your introductory sentences to signal the key themes you will develop
.
3. Infuse Humanity and Warmth
William Zinsser argues that "clutter" and "creeping nounism" are the diseases of business writing
.
Avoid "Corporate Robots": Do not use abstractions like "profitability" or "implementation"
. Instead, use active verbs and real nouns that allow the reader to visualize someone doing something
.
Locate the "I": A vision statement should reflect the identity and enthusiasm of the person behind it
. Zinsser suggests writing the first draft in the first person ("I" or "we") to keep the tone warm, even if you remove the pronouns later
.
Be Natural: Never say anything in writing that you wouldn't comfortably say in conversation
.
4. Apply the Principle of "Thinking Small"
To make a large vision engaging, don't try to be definitive or all-encompassing, which can lead to "unwieldy writing" that drains enthusiasm
.
Find the "Window": Like a memoir, a good vision statement shouldn't be the summary of an entire life or company; it should be a window into the intended future
.
One Provocative Thought: Every successful piece of nonfiction should leave the reader with exactly one provocative thought they didn't have before
.
5. Practice Ruthless Concision
A strong vision statement must be "stripped to its cleanest components"
.
Prune Redundancy: Eliminate "meaningless words" (like actually, basically, particular) and "redundant pairs" (like full and complete)
.
End with Strength: Your final sentence should encapsulate the vision and conclude with a jolt of fitness or surprise
.

> AGENT

Watchlist comes back clean — the earlier writing-refinement pass already stripped clutter, and the nominalizations that remain (`Supervision`/`Workflow` are the Layer *names*; `recording`/`grading`/`enforcement`/`observability` are the named project concerns) are load-bearing domain vocabulary, not creeping nounism. So §5 (concision) and §3's "creeping nounism" are essentially already satisfied.

The gaps are the **vision-specific** ones these guidelines add on top of `/writing-refinement` — SCQ order, the window, one provocative thought, and ending with strength. Here's the verdict.

## Verdict: accept with notes — 4 findings

**Finding 1 — SCQ is inverted (the big one). §1 opens with the Answer, not the Situation.**
Minto wants Situation → Complication → Question → Answer. The doc opens at line 7 with *"I want one toolbox…"* — that's the Answer. The Situation ("drain-cycle drains a Linear cycle unattended — one ticket, one worker, one diff") is buried in §2, and the Complication ("Today both halves are broken") lands *after* the Answer. The reader gets the resolution before the tension.
*Fix:* lead §1 with two sentences of known-state → disruption, then the question, then "I want one toolbox…". The material already exists in §2's "Where this came from" — pull its first clause up and trim the duplication.

> drain-cycle drains a Linear cycle unattended — one ticket, one worker, one diff. But the skills it drives still assume a human at the keyboard, the automation has drifted into re-implementing them as prompt prose, and the two have begun to diverge — so a worker can now finish the *work* and still strand the *ticket*, with nothing to catch it. The question this doc answers: what should the automation actually be? **One toolbox of skills I can run two ways without rebuilding anything —** …

**Finding 2 — the doc ends on a list, draining the close. §11 (Open Questions) is the last thing the reader sees.**
The guidelines want the final sentence to "encapsulate the vision and conclude with a jolt." A four-item open-questions list is the weakest possible ending for a *vision* (it's fine for an arch doc, which this also is). 
*Fix:* keep §11, but add a one-line coda after it that lands the thought — e.g. *"The whole design reduces to one move: stop watching each ticket, and trust the artifacts to tell me what watching used to."*

**Finding 3 — the "one provocative thought" is present but never stated once, sharply.**
Zinsser's window: the reader should leave with exactly one idea they didn't have. This doc's is strong and genuinely yours — *the orchestrator is me, automated, and the price is the live feedback loop, bought back with observability.* But it's currently spread across §1, §9, and §10. State it once, cleanly, where it can't be missed (end of §1 or the §10 heading).

**Finding 4 — "thinking small" tension is real but I'd mostly leave it.**
The guidelines warn against the all-encompassing, unwieldy form — and at 11 sections with tables, alternatives, and open questions, this *is* comprehensive. But you deliberately fused vision + architecture into one doc. My call: don't gut it. Make the **vision-bearing prose** (§1, §9–10, the new coda) obey thinking-small and warmth; let the **scaffolding** (§2–8, the tables, alternatives) stay as the reference half. Worth noting in the doc itself that the first screen is the window and the rest is the blueprint.

The strengths to preserve: the title (*"supervised, not babysat"* — that's the tension in four words), the consistently first-person voice (Zinsser's "locate the I," already done), and §7's concrete incident (palpable cost, exactly as the guidelines want).

Want me to apply Findings 1–3 (the SCQ reorder of §1, the closing coda, and stating the one thought once)? Those are the high-leverage edits; Finding 4 is a judgment call I'd lean toward leaving as-is.

- **Edit 2 (F2):** appends a closing coda so the doc ends on a jolt — *"stop watching each ticket, and trust the artifacts to tell me what watching used to"* — bookending the §1 thesis instead of ending on an open-questions list.

Problem\n\ndrain-cycle drains a Linear cycle unattended — one ticket, one worker, one diff. But the skills it drives still assume a human at the keyboard; the automation has drifted into re-implementing the workflow as prompt prose instead of pointing at those skills; and the two definitions have begun to diverge. The failure that makes the drift concrete: a worker can finish the *work* and still not finish the *ticket*, and nothing catches it (§7).\n\nSo the question this doc answers is what the automation should actually *be*. The answer: **one toolbox of skills I run two ways without rebuilding anything.**\n\n- **By hand** — invoke a single discrete skill at the keyboard when I want exactly that one step (`/shape:design`, `/exec:review`, `/shape:pr-finishing`).\n- **Unattended** — point an engine at a Linear project and have it run those same skills in the right sequence, ticket by ticket, to a merged reviewable PR — without me watching every step.\n\nIn both modes the **same skills** are the unit of work, and the automation is a thin layer that does what I do by hand as a supervisor — start the work, hold the guardrails, check the result. **The real goal is to replace my own mechanical supervision with an orchestrator** so my attention moves to higher-order work — scoping the next project, validating delivered work, improving the system — instead of watching each ticket run.\n\nThat autonomy comes at a price I take on deliberately: **automating supervision trades away the live feedback loop.** Supervising by hand, I catch a workflow going wrong early and I pivot fast; an orchestrated run gives that up — it executes to a halt or a Done and I inspect afterward. So I apply autonomy selectively, on work where the trade pays, and I lean on observability to buy back a delayed version of the loop — which is also how I improve the product over time.\n\nThe whole design reduces to one bet: **the orchestrator is me, automated — and the single thing I give up, the live feedback loop, I buy back through observability.** Everything below follows from it.\n```\n\n**Why:** Situation (drains a cycle unattended) → Complication (skills assume a human, automation\ndrifted, they diverge, work strands) → Question (what should the automation *be*) → Answer (one\ntoolbox, two modes) — Minto order.   and Complication arrive afterward. Reorder to Situation → Complication → Question → Answer.

  close on a jolt. Add a short coda after §11.
- **F3 — the one provocative thought is never stated once, sharply.** It's spread across §1, §9,
  §10. State it once at the end of §1 (the window / first screen), echoed compressed in the F2 coda.

F4 (thinking-small / the doc is comprehensive) is deliberately left as-is: vision prose obeys
the rules; the §2–8 scaffolding stays as the reference blueprint.

actor **drain-cycle** instead of the vague abstraction "the automation" (3 uses → 1), kept

"That autonomy comes at a price…" paragraph) with:

```markdown
## 1. Problem

drain-cycle drains a Linear cycle unattended — one ticket, one worker, one diff. But the skills it drives still assume a human at the keyboard; drain-cycle has drifted into re-implementing their workflow as prompt prose rather than pointing at the skills themselves; and the two definitions have begun to diverge. The failure that makes the drift concrete: a worker can finish the *work* and still not finish the *ticket*, and nothing catches it (§7).

So the real question is how the skills and the automation should relate. The answer: **the skills are one toolbox I run two ways without rebuilding anything**, and drain-cycle sits on top as a thin supervisor — never a second copy of the workflow.

- **By hand** — invoke a single discrete skill at the keyboard when I want exactly that one step (`/shape:design`, `/exec:review`, `/shape:pr-finishing`).
- **Unattended** — point drain-cycle at a Linear project and have it run those same skills in the right sequence, ticket by ticket, to a merged reviewable PR — without me watching every step.

The supervisor does what I do by hand: start the work, hold the guardrails, check the result. **The real goal is to replace my own mechanical supervision with an orchestrator** so my attention moves to higher-order work — scoping the next project, validating delivered work, improving the system — instead of watching each ticket run.

That autonomy comes at a price I take on deliberately: **automating supervision trades away the live feedback loop.** Supervising by hand, I catch a workflow going wrong early and pivot fast; an orchestrated run gives that up — it executes to a halt or a Done and I inspect afterward. So I apply autonomy selectively, on work where the trade pays, and lean on observability to buy back a delayed version of the loop — which is also how I improve the product over time.

The whole design reduces to one bet: **the orchestrator is me, automated — and the single thing I give up, the live feedback loop, I buy back through observability.** Everything below follows from it.
```

**Why:** Situation (drains a cycle unattended) → Complication (skills assume a human, drain-cycle
drifted, the two diverge, work strands) → Question (how should the skills and the automation
relate) → Answer (one toolbox, two modes; drain-cycle a thin supervisor) — Minto order. The final
bolded line is F3: the one provocative thought, stated once, in the stress position.

### Edit 1b — de-duplicate §2's opening (follows from Edit 1)

Edit 1 moves the phrase *"drain a Linear cycle unattended — one ticket, one worker, one diff"*
into §1. §2 currently opens by repeating it verbatim:

> **Where this came from.** `drain-cycle` began as "drain a Linear cycle unattended — one ticket, one worker, one diff." Three threads then pulled it forward: …

Change to avoid the echo:

**Append** after the last open-question line (end of file):

```markdown

The bet is small enough to say in one line and large enough to reorganize the whole project around: **stop watching each ticket, and trust the artifacts to tell me what watching used to.**
```

**Why:** ends on a jolt instead of a list, and names the artifact boundary (the doc's
architectural spine) as the compressed echo of the §1 thesis — bookending the one thought rather
than introducing a second. Vocab pass dropped the "window / blueprint" sentence — "blueprint" is
on the `WRITING.md` watchlist, and "window" was meta-commentary on the writing guideline that a
reader of the vision has no context for.

---

1. **One thought, bookended vs. once.** Edit 1 states the thesis at end of §1; Edit 2 echoes it
   compressed at the very end. If you'd rather state it exactly once, drop the §1 line and keep
   only the coda (or vice-versa). Default: keep both as bookends.
2. **§2 trim (Edit 1b)** is only needed because Edit 1 lifts that phrase up. If you keep §2
   verbatim, the phrase repeats once — minor.

19

Decision §17 ("`/shape:task` runs inside the worker session") is the high-water mark of the *old* inline-workflow approach and is superseded by the boundary below.
